國際經濟金融週報內容摘要 
美 
國 
企業活動維持擴張，消費信心與房市疲弱，通膨及關稅風險仍存 
經濟 
⚫ 10 月美國綜合 PMI 產出指數初值由 58.4 降至 57.6，企業活動仍強勁擴
張，內需及出口帶動訂單與就業成長；惟油價、關稅及供應鏈瓶頸推升
成本，售價漲幅創 2022 年 8 月以來新高，通膨壓力持續升溫，後續須
關注聯準會政策動向。 
⚫ 9 月美國住宅市場續顯疲弱，建築許可及新屋開工分別年減 3.0%及
7.4%，反映住宅投資動能放緩。儘管多戶住宅許可與完工數回升，但整
體營建活動仍偏弱，後續仍須關注房貸利率、住房負擔能力及營建成本
對市場復甦的影響。 
⚫ 密西根大學公布之 10 月美國消費者信心指數降至 48.0，月減 0.2%、年
減 10.4%，顯示民眾對目前經濟環境仍缺乏信心，雖然對未來景氣的看
法略有改善，但高物價與借貸成本仍讓消費者感到壓力。此外，未來一
年及長期通膨預期分別升至 4.7%及 3.5%，反映民眾仍擔心物價持續上
漲。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ 10 月 23 日川普宣布調整加拿大商品 50%關稅清單，10 月 30 日生效，
豁免部分紙製品、機械及包裝材料，並新增地毯、油漆等課稅項目。外
媒認為此舉與期中選舉有關，有助降低部分企業成本，但美加貿易摩擦
仍未化解。 
短評 
⚫ 10 月美國綜合產出指數雖略為下滑，但仍維持高檔，顯示內需與就業持
續支撐企業活動擴張。然而，消費者信心仍處低檔，反映高物價與借貸
成本持續壓抑購買意願，儘管民眾對未來景氣的看法略有改善，但新屋
開工及建築許可同步下滑，顯示消費與住宅投資前景仍面臨挑戰。另一
方面，美國調整部分加拿大商品關稅，雖有助減輕部分企業進口成本，
但雙邊貿易摩擦尚未化解，政策不確定性仍存。

事件 
⚫ 歐洲議會代表團於 10 月 27 日至 29 日訪問中國，料將討論稀土及綠能

的妥協法案或者公共支出。部分較為悲觀的機構認為此波動盪可能會延
續至 2027 年 4 月的法國總統大選。 
⚫ 市場預期歐洲央行在 10 月 28 日的政策會議將採取觀望立場。

本 
貿易逆差及通膨壓力延續，內需仍具韌性 
經濟 
⚫ 9 月出口年增 16.7%。 
⚫ 9 月全國消費者物價指數(CPI)年增 2.1%。 
⚫ 10 月綜合 PMI 持平於 52.3。 
短評 
⚫ 出口維持強勁、服務需求亦持續擴張，惟中東局勢推升能源進口成本，
使貿易逆差擴大，並加重國內通膨壓力。隨成本上漲逐步由商品擴散至
服務價格，通膨黏著性可能進一步提高，為日本央行貨幣政策正常化提
供支撐；另一方面，原物料供應受限及投入成本居高不下，已開始影響
製造業動能與企業信心，後續仍須關注高成本環境對經濟成長的制約。

國 
第三季經濟成長率回升，但前景仍較為不利

國際經濟金融週報 
兆豐國際商業銀行 徵信處 
115.10.27 
一、 美國—企業活動維持擴張，消費信心與房市疲弱，通膨及關稅風險仍存 
標普全球(S&P Global)公布，2026 年 10 月美國綜合 PMI 產出指數(Composite 
PMI Output Index)初值由上月的 58.4 降至 57.6，其中服務業採購經理人指數
(Services PMI)由 58.8 降至 57.9，製造業採購經理人指數(Manufacturing PMI)則由
55.9 微升至 56.0，創 2022 年 5 月以來新高，企業活動雖略有放緩，但仍維持強
勁擴張。需求方面，內需穩健及出口改善帶動整體新訂單增速創逾 4 年半新高，
商品出口訂單亦為 2025 年 6 月以來首次增加；企業持續擴增人力，整體就業增
速為 2022 年 6 月以來次高。另一方面，未完成訂單增加、供應商交貨延遲加劇，
加上油價上漲及關稅推升能源、運輸與原物料成本，使企業投入成本維持高檔，
帶動售價漲幅升至 2022 年 8 月以來最高。儘管企業對未來一年營運前景的樂觀
程度略降，但仍處於今年相對高檔。整體而言，美國經濟在內外需及就業支撐下
維持擴張，惟供應鏈瓶頸與成本上升加劇通膨壓力，後續仍須關注物價走勢及聯
準會貨幣政策動向。 
密西根大學公布，2026 年 10 月美國消費者信心指數終值降至 48.0，略低於
上月的 48.1，月減 0.2%、年減 10.4%，並較今年 2 月下降約 15%，顯示消費者信
心持續處於低檔。分項來看，當前經濟狀況指數由 50.9 降至 46.7，月減 8.3%、
年減 20.3%；消費者預期指數則由 46.3 回升至 48.8，月增 5.4%、年減 3.0%，反
映民眾對未來景氣的看法略有改善，但整體信心仍偏弱。調查指出，高利率及購
買負擔壓力使耐久財購買條件下降 18%，拖累當前經濟狀況指數；未來一年企業
景氣預期雖改善約 14%，但仍低於去年同期 16%。通膨預期方面，未來一年通膨
預期由 4.6%升至 4.7%，長期通膨預期亦由 3.4%升至 3.5%，雙雙連續第二個月攀
升，創今年 5 月以來最高水準。密西根大學指出，2025 年關稅政策衝擊及 2026
年伊朗衝突引發的能源價格上漲，使民眾通膨預期再度升溫，且長期預期已明顯
高於 2019 年及 2024 年水準。整體而言，10 月美國消費者信心大致持平，但高物
價與借貸成本仍壓抑購買意願，加上短期及長期通膨預期持續上升，可能增加物
價回穩的難度，後續仍須關注能源價格、家庭消費及通膨預期變化對聯準會貨幣
政策的影響。

5

2026 年 9 月美國住宅市場建築許可年化總數降至 140.0 萬戶，月減 0.2%、年
減 3.0%，其中單戶住宅建築許可降至 84.5 萬戶，月減 4.0%、年減 4.8%，惟 5 戶
以上多戶住宅建築許可增至 50.3 萬戶，月增 5.9%、年增 1.0%，顯示不同住宅類
型開發規劃表現分歧。新屋開工年化數降至 122.1 萬戶，月減 5.2%、年減 7.4%，
其中單戶住宅開工 85.9 萬戶，較去年同期仍增 2.6%，但多戶住宅開工降至 34.9
萬戶，年減 25.9%，拖累整體營建動能。施工方面，在建住宅維持 128.0 萬戶，與
上月持平但較去年減少 3.3%；完工年化數則回升至 119.2 萬戶，月增 2.2%，主要
受多戶住宅完工月增 14.9%帶動，惟整體仍較去年同期減少 14.0%。整體而言，
美國住宅市場呈現建築許可及新屋開工下滑、在建規模持平及完工略有回升的態
勢，反映住宅營建活動仍偏弱，後續仍須關注房貸利率走勢、住房負擔能力及營
建成本變化對住宅市場復甦的影響。 
美國總統川普 10 月 23 日依據《1930 年關稅法》第 338 條簽署公告，調整部
分加拿大商品加徵 50%關稅的適用範圍，並自 10 月 30 日起生效。此次調整取消
膠合板、部分紙製品、機械與冷凍設備、包裝用木箱及紙箱、新鮮與乾燥海藻等
商品的額外關稅，同時新增閥門、地毯、油漆、洗髮精、可可粉、木製棺材、空
調設備及獨木舟等課稅項目，呈現局部鬆綁與新增課稅並行的政策方向。白宮表
示，此舉旨在減輕加拿大貿易措施對美國企業造成的負擔，並維護國內產業利益。
由於緬因州農業及製造業高度依賴加拿大進口物資，共和黨參議員柯林斯(Susan 
Collins)積極向白宮爭取關稅豁免，外媒亦將此次調整與 11 月 3 日期中選舉連結。
整體而言，新措施有助降低部分美國企業的進口成本，但美加貿易摩擦尚未完全
化解，後續仍須關注雙邊談判及關稅政策對供應鏈的影響。 
二、歐元區—法國動盪短期內難降溫，料將使央行保持審慎觀望態度 
 歐元區 10 月綜合採購經理人指數(PMI)下降 1.3 點至 51.9，抵銷上月的強勁
增幅，但為連續五個月高於 50 的榮枯線，主要係受到法國大規模抗議活動的影
響，使法國綜合 PMI 下降 2.7 點至 48.4；此外，德國綜合 PMI 下降 1 點至 52.8，
亦反映近期通膨壓力增加，使民眾傾向保守消費。若以產業區分，歐元區服務業、
製造業 PMI 分別降 1.3、0.5 點至 51.7、52.9；惟德國製造業 PMI 上升至相當強勁
的 54.2，反映德國政府擴張軍備投資以及基礎建設之需求，持續推動製造業生產
動能。

6

法國抗爭活動規模逐漸擴大，各大工會與抗議團體已宣布在 10 月 31 日再度
發起大規模遊行，並預告於 11 月 5 日舉行全國性大罷工，抗議主要訴求包括改
善公共服務品質及凍結物價上漲。然而，在財政壓力巨大且國會缺乏穩定執政多
數的情況下，法國政府既無法強力通過重大的財政改革，也難以快速推出安撫反
對派的妥協法案或者公共支出。故部分較為悲觀的機構認為此波動盪可能會延續
至 2027 年 4 月的法國總統大選。受法國局勢影響，近期歐元區各國殖利率普遍
呈現上升趨勢，並對景氣產生暫時性的抑制效果。在此背景下，市場預期歐洲央
行在 10 月 28 日的政策會議將採取觀望立場，後續關注歐洲央行是否將透過政策
訊號或流動性工具，為平息法國主權債務利率而採取行動。 
三、日本—貿易逆差及通膨壓力延續，內需仍具韌性 
 9 月出口雖維持強勁，受半導體製造設備及汽車出口增加所帶動，出口額年增
16.7%；惟進口增幅更為顯著，9 月進口額年增 18.9%，其中原油及液化天然氣進
口額分別大增 51.1%及 39.2%，主要反映中東局勢推升能源價格，以及日本尋求
替代能源供應來源的影響。由於進口額高於出口額，9 月貿易逆差達 5,365 億日
圓，為連續第 5 個月呈現逆差；累計 2026 上半財年（4 至 9 月）貿易逆差亦較上
年同期擴大 98.8%至 2.82 兆日圓。整體而言，出口並未明顯轉弱，近期貿易逆差
擴大主要反映能源進口成本攀升，使進口增幅持續高於出口。 
 9 月全國消費者物價指數(CPI)年增 2.1%，即使政府透過能源及教育補貼等措
施抑制物價，通膨仍高於 2%，顯示實際通膨動能更為強勁。從物價結構來看，石
油、原物料及工資成本上升已逐步傳導至終端價格，漲價範圍由家具、醫療用品
及休閒用品等商品，進一步擴散至房屋維護、公共運輸、住宿及外食等服務項目，
顯示企業成本轉嫁範圍逐步擴大，通膨壓力亦由商品向服務領域擴散。隨通膨持
續高於 2%且漲價範圍擴大，亦為日本央行進一步推動貨幣政策正常化提供支撐。 
 10 月綜合 PMI 持平於 52.3，顯示經濟仍維持穩定擴張，其中製造業 PMI 下
降 0.8 點至 53.3，產出指數亦降至 3 月以來最低，主要反映新訂單及出口訂單成
長放緩，加上稀土金屬等原料供應受限，對生產形成一定壓力，惟生產與訂單仍
處擴張區間，尚未顯示製造業景氣明顯轉弱；服務業 PMI 回升 0.7 點至 52.0，新
業務及出口訂單同步改善，顯示服務需求仍具韌性，為支撐景氣的重要動能。另
一方面，能源、薪資及原物料成本居高不下，使投入價格壓力持續偏高，並逐漸

### Page 8

用相關內容。

影響企業對未來景氣的信心。 
四、中國—第三季經濟成長率回升，但前景仍較為不利 
2026 年第三季 GDP 年增率初估值為 4.5%，較第二季的 4.3%上揚 0.2 個百分
點；累計 1-9 月 GDP 年增率則為 4.6%。第三季的二、三級產業年增率分別較第
二季上揚 0.4、0.1 個百分點至 3.4%，其中製造業、批發零售業年增率分別達 5.9%、
2.3%為主要動能，抵銷建築業年減 4.4%等拖累因素影響。整體而言，第三季 GDP
年增率上揚有部分受到中秋國慶連假落在 9 月末段(去年同期則於 10 月 1 日開
始)，致基期效果發揮部分影響，相關消費動能仍可能會在 10 月出現暫時性的回
落。 
在需求指標方面，(1)1-9 月累計零售額年增率為 1.1%，持平於 1-8 月的累計
年增率。其中 9 月零售額年增率增加 0.7 個百分點至 1.1%，部分受到新手機上市
帶動買氣，9 月通訊器材消費年增率高達 31.1%，加上中秋國慶長假帶動食品消
費年增率增加 1 個百分點至 2.1%所致；(2)1-9 月固定資產投資年減幅維持於 7.2%，
反映供應鏈移轉、政府主導去化過剩產能等因素導致民間投資需求急遽萎縮；萎
縮產業包含化工、醫藥、專用設備、汽車製造業、金屬製品業等；(3)1-9 月房地
產開發投資額年減幅由 19.9%擴大至 20.6%、施工面積年減幅為 12.8%，新建商
品房銷售面積年減幅擴大為 13.2%，房市投資、買氣皆持續低迷。 
受到出口維持高度成長帶動，9 月工業生產年增率較 8 月增加 0.3 個百分點
至 5.5%(累計 1-9 月工業生產年增率持平 1-8 月水準為 5.3%)。依產業別來看，9
月主要受到煤礦開採、化工、化纖等產業擴張影響，主因中秋與國慶長假前發電
業充實庫存水位，促使煤炭企業加快復產，加上油價上漲帶動相關中下游供應鏈
增產；惟高科技產品包含 IC、機器人、新能源車等年增率皆有放慢，反映去年中
秋國慶連假皆落在 10 月，使得去年 9 月工作日較多，致基期相對墊高的影響。 
展望未來，儘管政府 10 月初宣布釋放 5,500 億元人民幣的地方政府債務結存
限額，但額度下達到資金撥付往往需要數月時間，預期對於第四季的經濟影響有
限，使今年全年經濟成長率預估將落在 4.5%附近。此外，受歐盟右派勢力崛起及
保護主義影響，歐盟對中國貿易政策可能持續趨嚴，後續仍須關注雙方談判及溝
通結果，未來若外需動能減弱，預期中國政府將持續透過發展特定高科技產業及
扶植內需來支持經濟成長動能，以減輕房市不振及貿易環境惡化的衝擊。

8

五、印度及東南亞—新、馬出口保持強勁增速，菲律賓央行大舉升息 
(1)印度: 10 月 HSBC 綜合採購經理人指數（PMI）初值上升 0.8 點至 56.7，其中，
製造業 PMI 略下滑 0.2 點至 54.9、服務業 PMI 則上揚 1.0 點至 56.2，皆保持穩健
擴張態勢。由於美國、英國、中東及孟加拉等地的訂單增加，帶動新訂單及出口
訂單加速成長，製造商採購庫存增速創八個月來最快、成品庫存增速亦創歷史新
高，然而聘僱指數降至今年以來最低、未完成訂單下降，反映現階段人力充足。
另一方面，投入成本漲幅連續第七個月下降並降至近一年低點，產品售價漲幅亦
明顯放緩，通膨壓力緩解，有助提升企業獲利能力及消費者購買力，並且給予印
度央行（RBI）貨幣政策空間。 
(2)新加坡：9 月非石油國內出口（NODX）年增 68.4%，增速史上最快，連續第
23 個月正成長，其中，人工智慧需求強勁，帶動電子產品出口躍增 107.6%；非電
子產品出口亦大幅成長 54.2%，主要由船舶和遊艇組件所帶動。按出口目的地劃
分，對香港出口激增 121.1%、對台灣、韓國出口增幅達 96%，對美出口成長 73.2%，
但對印度出口下降 5.6%。展望未來，新加坡金融管理局在 10 月的宏觀經濟評估
報告中表示，直至 2027 年人工智慧 (AI) 驅動的成長將保持相對穩定，支持未
來幾季經濟成長表現。由於基數較高以及人工智慧相關投資預計放緩，全球利率
上升可能會對新加坡主要貿易夥伴的消費和非人工智慧投資造成壓力，可能會影
響外部需求，薪資成長放緩、招聘謹慎以及運輸成本上升可能會在 2026 年下半
年對可自由支配支出和零售活動構成壓力，預計 2027 年 GDP 成長將放緩。 
(3)印尼：印尼央行（BI）10 月會議連續第四次會議維持基準利率(7 天期逆回購利
率,BI-Rate)於 5.75%不變，貸款便利利率（LFR）維持在 6.50%不變，但將隔夜存
款便利利率（DFR）調高 25 個基點至 5%，旨在提高印尼盾資產的回報率，藉此
吸引並留住外資，同時宣布分兩階段調降存款準備率，11 月 1 日起下調 1 個百分
點至 8%，12 月 1 日起進一步降至 7%，以提供銀行體系流動性。整體而言，印尼
央行爲捍衛印尼盾，在 5 至 6 月間共升息 100 個基點，同時調降外資避險成本，
並採取「資金停留愈久、優惠愈高」的機制吸引外資流入。惟近期國際指數編纂
公司 MSCI 擬將印尼股市由”新興市場”降級為”獨立市場”，引發資金外流疑慮，
央行選擇利率按兵不動，搭配降準提供銀行體系流動性，以兼顧印尼盾穩定與國
內經濟需求，未來 MSCI 檢討進展(最快 2027 年 4 月公布)及印尼盾走勢，將持續
牽動央行後續政策。

(4)菲律賓：菲律賓央行（BSP）10 月 22 日宣布大幅調升基準利率(逆回購利率)2
碼至 5.50%，為 2023 年以來最大幅度升息。央行指出， 9 月通膨率已升至 7.2%，
遠高於 2%-4%的目標區間；同時核心通膨持續攀升，顯示價格上漲已由食品與能
源擴散至整體經濟。BSP 亦擔憂中東情勢推升國際油價、運輸費率調漲，以及聖
嬰現象衝擊農業生產，進一步推高物價。此外，披索貶至歷史低點附近，增加進
口通膨風險，因此採取較激進的緊縮措施以穩定市場預期，貨幣政策重點已由支
持經濟成長轉向優先遏制通膨風險。 
(5)馬來西亞：9 月出口額年增 54.5%至 MYR2,151 億元，其中電子&電氣產品出
口大增 69.2%，持續反映 AI 帶動的強勁需求，礦產品出口亦大幅成長 56.3%，抵
銷農產品出口續跌 5.7%的影響，對台灣、美國出口大增 175.2%、89.9%、對新加
坡出口增幅亦達 50.4%。9 月進口額成長 52.7%至 MYR1,814 億元，受惠出口引
申需求，中間產品、資本貨物進口分別成長 69.6%、20.9%，消費品進口轉為正成
長 18.2%，自中國和新加坡進口分別成長 67.6%、90.7%。進出口相抵，9 月貿易
順差達 MYR337 億元、年增 64.9%。 
六、台灣—外銷訂單創新高，產業景氣冷熱不均 
9 月外銷訂單達 1,142.1 億美元，再創單月新高，年增 62.4%，主要受惠於人
工智慧、高效能運算與雲端服務等應用蓬勃發展，以及消費性電子新品陸續推出，
帶動相關供應鏈維持高檔；加上國際原物料價格上漲，推升整體接單表現。惟各
產業接單動能仍呈分化，科技產品需求強勁，傳統產業景氣則相對疲弱。經濟部
指出，雖有年底節慶補貨潮挹注，但傳統產業部分產能過剩，終端需求仍未見明
顯回升。

2026 年 7-9 月期 GDP 予測(最終版) 
～前期比年率▲0.4％の小幅マイナス成長を予想～ 
経済調査部 シニアエグゼクティブエコノミスト 新家義貴 
（℡：050-5474-7490）

11 月16 日に公表される26 年７-９月期の実質
ＧＤＰ成長率を前期比年率▲0.4％（前期比▲
0.1％）と予測する。10 月 30 日の段階では前期
比年率＋0.4％（前期比＋0.1％）と小幅プラス
成長を予想していたが、その後公表された経済
指標の結果を反映し、小幅マイナス成長へと予
測値を下方修正する。 
本日公表された26 年９月分の国際収支統計の
結果を反映し、26 年７-９月期の実質輸出を前期
比＋0.1％（従来予測値：同＋1.2％）へ下方修
正する。筆者の９月分の想定と比べてサービス
輸出が大きく下振れたことが主因である。実質
輸入も前期比＋3.0％（従来予測値：同＋
3.3％）と下方修正するが、輸出の下方修正度合
いの方が大きいことから、外需の予測値を前期
比年率寄与度▲1.9％Pt（従来予測値：同▲
1.3％Pt）へ下方修正している。 
また、９月分の家計調査と家計消費状況調査
が想定よりも下振れたことを反映し、個人消費
の予測値も前期比▲0.3％と、従来予測値の同▲
0.2％から小幅下方修正した。

物価高による下押しや耐久財需要の反動に加え、天候不順の影響もあって個人消費が減少する
ことが成長率を押し下げる見込みである。特に、８～９月には長雨・大雨や台風などの天候不順
が相次ぎ、外出手控えや旅行・宿泊の見送りなどを通じて消費が下押しされたとみられる。一
方、設備投資は高い伸びとなり、景気を下支えするとみられる。設備投資の伸びには特殊要因も

2/2
一部含まれているが、それを除いても堅調だったと考えられる。
７-９月期は４四半期ぶりのマイナス成長になると予想するが、マイナス幅は大きくない。今回
のマイナス成長には天候不順という一時的な下押し要因も含まれており、過去３四半期に高い成
長が続いた後であることや、企業部門の底堅さが維持されていることを踏まえれば、景気回復の
流れが途切れたと判断する必要はないだろう。
物価高による節約志向は根強く、個人消費の回復にはなお時間を要するとみられるが、設備投
資を中心に企業部門が好調さを維持している点は好材料である。家計部門が力強さを欠く一方、
企業部門が景気を下支えする構図のもと、日本経済は均してみれば緩やかな回復基調にあると判
断している。

Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
4Q26 iPhone builds unchanged: We maintain our 4Q26 iPhone build forecast at
72M units (+33% q/q, -5% y/y). While builds are expected to increase sequentially,
volumes remain below last year’s level, with softer legacy-model production,
including the iPhone 16/17e, weighing on the outlook. Our 2H26 build forecast for

the new iPhone 18 family remains unchanged at 71M units (-21% y/y), with the y/y
comparison also affected by one fewer SKU launched than last year. Within this, we
continue to expect 7M units of iPhone Duo builds. We will continue to monitor the
production ramp and sell-through data for potential upside or downside to our build
estimates. Our full-year forecast implies CY26 iPhone shipments of 234M units
(+3% y/y), with modest growth for the year despite the softer 4Q y/y outlook.

4Q26 iPad builds unchanged; year-end production outlook remains subdued: We
maintain our 4Q26 iPad build forecast at 10.3M units (-10% q/q, -26% y/y), implying
continued pressure on production volumes into year-end. Our full-year estimate
remains 46.8M units (-14% y/y), with the sharper y/y contraction in 4Q pointing to a
weaker finish to the year.

communications with a subject company, public appearances
and trading securities held by a research analyst account.
Key Takeaways
4Q26e iPhone build unchanged at 72M units (+33% q/q, -5% y/y).
4Q26e iPad build unchanged at 10.3M units (-10% q/q, -26% y/y).

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views

below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

## Document 147: 261117_daiwa_merida

Source PDF: all report pdf/261117_daiwa_merida.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-11-17
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Summary: Merida hosted its 3Q26 investor call on 17 November after market hours, 
following release of its 3Q26 results on 11 November (see our memo). Merida’s gross 
margin came in at 15.2% (vs. 19.8% in 2Q26; 13.7% in 3Q25), lower than our estimate 
(16.2%) and the consensus (17.3%). Factoring in the equity investment loss of 
TWD150m+, its 3Q26 EPS was TWD1.06, lower than our forecast (TWD1.68) and the 
consensus estimate (TWD1.65).

We have a Buy (1) rating with a 12-month TP of TWD96, based on a target PER of 17x 
applied on our 1-year-forward EPS forecast. For more information on this company, please 
refer to our latest flash note, 2Q26 review: mainly dragged by Specialized, on 14 August.

What’s the impact 
• 3Q26 results recap. Merida’s 3Q26 revenue of TWD6,098m (-12.1% QoQ, -15.6% 
YoY) missed our and the Bloomberg consensus estimates by 13.6% and 11.1%, 
respectively, which we attributed to the discount when destocking the older models. Its 
gross margin came in at 15.2% (vs. 19.8% in 2Q26; 13.7% in 3Q25), lower than our 
estimate (16.2%) and the consensus (17.3%), due to less OBM sales, the price discount 
for further inventory digestion and the depreciation of the Euro. Factoring in the equity 
investment loss of TWD150m+, its net profit of TWD176m (-44.8% QoQ, -71.9% YoY) 
missed our and the consensus estimates by 65.0% and 65.6%. Its 3Q26 EPS was 
TWD1.06, lower than our forecast (TWD1.68) and the consensus estimate (TWD1.65).  
• 2026 outlook update. Merida revised down its 2026 full-year shipments to 960k units 
(Taiwan plant 280k, down from previous 340k; China plant 680k, down from previous 
700k), as it continues to manage its existing model shipments and expects new models 
in 2027 to bring stronger demand. The company now sees recovery in Europe and 
China, with inventory digestion coming to an end: 9M26 sales in Europe grew 14% YoY, 
driven by strong demand for road bikes and gravel bikes; 9M 26 sales in China grew by 
1% while shipments grew by 7% (vs. revenue guidance of declining 15-20% at the 
beginning of 2026), driven by low- and mid-end models. 
• SBC expected to be profitable in 4Q26. Due to SBC’s conservative tax assumption 
(over 100% effective tax rate) in 1Q-3Q26, if and when the tax rate realised at year-end 
is determined to be less than currently allocated for, we expect there will be a huge profit 
recognised in 4Q26 to offset the estimated after-tax loss recognised in 1Q-3Q26. Merida 
and SBC will review the tax assumption policy after full-year results are out, as the 
policy has caused a great deal of investor confusion. Including USD15m of tariff refund 
in 3Q26, SBC’s pre-tax profit grew by 42% YoY for 9M26. Merida expects there is 
another USD5m of tariff refund to collect in 4Q26.

What we recommend 
We have a Buy (1) rating with a 12-month TP of TWD96, based on a target PER of 17x 
applied on our 1-year-forward EPS forecast. The stock is currently trading at PERs of 
13.2x/10.6x, based on our 2026/27E EPS, vs. its past-5-year one-year-forward PER 
trading range of 7-33x. Key downside risks: slower-than-expected e-bike penetration in the 
EU and the US, worse-than-expected demand in an inflationary environment and inventory 
digestion.

In the interests of timeliness, this document has not been edited.

Country

## Document 215: [000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다실적에 수급을 더한다 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다실적에 수급을 더한다 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
여전히 가격 상승 구간 
SK하이닉스의 26년 3분기 매출액은 95.8조원, 영업이익 75.1조원으로 예상한다. 
이전 전망 대비 감소한 수준이다. 원/달러 환율 영향이다. 영업이익률은 78.4%로 전 
분기 대비 2.1%p 상승할 전망이다. DRAM 매출액은 73.1조원, 영업이익 
59.2조원으로 추정한다. DRAM ASP는 이전 전망 대비 상향 조정했다. 일부 제품 
가격 인상을 반영한 영향이다. Bit Growth는 이전 전망 수준을 유지했다. ASP 
상향이 수익성 개선에 미친 영향은 크지 않을 전망이다. NAND 매출액은 22.4조원, 
영업이익은 15.9조원으로 예상한다. 
Global Top 수준 주주환원 규모/자사주 살 게 더 있다 
SK하이닉스는 2026년 8월 19일 주주환원에 대한 새로운 정책을 제시했다. FCF에서 
CAPEX를 제외한 금액의 50% 이상을 주주환원에 사용한다는게 핵심이다. 2026년 
기준으로 최소 90조원 수준일 것으로 추정한다. 자사주 중심으로 주주환원을 계획 
중이고 이미 집행 중인 40조원 이외에 4분기 중에 추가로 자사주 매입이 진행될 
것으로 예상한다. 배당도 일부 집행이 되겠지만 자사주가 중심이 될 전망이다. 
흔들리지 말아야 할 이유는 많다 
SK하이닉스에 대한 투자의견은 매수를 유지한다. AI 모델의 성장으로 메모리에 
대한 수요가 지속적으로 높아지고 있고, 이에 비해 공급 증가는 제한적인 상황이 
상당기간 지속될 것으로 전망하기 때문이다. 이로 인해 현재 수준의 DRAM, NAND 
가격에 변동성은 낮고, 높은 수익성이 유지되면서 안정적 현금흐름 창출이 가능할 
것으로 전망한다. 현재 주가는 지나치게 저평가되어 있는 국면에 있다고 판단한다. 
목표주가는 12개월 예상 EPS 446,092원에 PER 9배를 적용한 4,000,000원을 
유지한다.

Compliance Notice 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 외부의 압력이나 부당한 간섭없이 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하여 작성되었음을 확인합니다. 
동 자료는 기관투자가 또는 제3자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다. 
동 자료는 조사분석자료 작성에 참여한 외부인(계열회사 및 그 임직원등)이 없습니다. 
조사분석 담당자 및 배우자는 해당종목과 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 
동 자료에 언급된 종목의 지분율 1%이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
당사는 상기 명시한 사항 외 고지해야 하는 특별한 이해관계가 없습니다.

종목명 담당자 담당자(배우자) 보유여부 1%이상 보유여부 유가증권 발행관련 계열사 관계여부 공개매수 사무취급 IPO 회사채 지급보증 중대한 이해관계 M&A 관련 수량 취득가 취득일 
해당 사항 없음

3Q26 Review: 악재의 중첩, 
4분기까지 지켜보자 
최정환 CFA

jeonghwan.choi@daishin.com

투자의견 BUY  
매수, 유지 
6개월 목표주가 1,640,000 
유지  
현재주가 
(26.10.29) 
739,000 
방위산업

◼ 투자의견 매수, 목표주가 1,640,000원 유지 
◼ 컨센서스 및 추정치 대비 매출, 영업이익 하회 
◼ 4Q26 및 27년 실적 추정치 상향

투자의견 매수, 목표주가 1,640,000원 유지 
4Q26E-2Q27E 지상방산 및 항공우주 EBITDA에 Target 12MF EV/EBITDA 25.0x 
적용, 상장 자회사 가치 SOTP 통해 목표주가 산출. 3Q26 실적 아쉬우나 투자포
인트에는 변함 없어 멀티플은 변경 없이 적용. 27년 실적 추정치 상향에 따라 본
업 가치 상승, 26년 스페인 자주포 양산 및 미국 자주포 시제 사업자로 선정되며 
현지화 전략이 유효했음을 증명. 3Q26 실적에 대한 우려를 소화하며 글로벌 피어
들과의 멀티플 격차 축소를 전망함

컨센서스 및 추정치 대비 매출, 영업이익 하회 
3Q26 연결 기준 매출액 6.1조원(-6.0% YoY), 영업이익 4,520억원(-46.2% YoY, 
OPM 7.4%)으로 컨센서스인 매출액 7.4조원 및 영업이익 1.1조원을 크게 하회
하는 실적 시현. 하회의 주요 요인으로는 3Q26 필리조선소 적자폭 확대, 지상
방산 부문 실적이 부진한 영향. 지상방산 부문의 경우 매출액 1.2조원(-41.0% 
YoY), 영업이익 220억원(-96.2% YoY, OPM 1.7%)을 시현했는데, 폴란드 관련 
매출이 부재했던 1Q26 영업이익률 16.9%보다도 낮은 실적을 보임. 이는 국내
외 사업 모두에서 악재가 겹쳐 발생했던 것으로 보이는데 국내 사업에서는 마
진율이 낮은 사업이 매출로 인식되었고 수출 사업에서는 폴란드 매출 부재 뿐 
아니라 이집트, 호주 K9 자주포 매출 인식 또한 예상 대비 부진해 고정비 레버
리지 효과로 3Q26 영업이익률이 크게 훼손된 것으로 추정. 이외 대전사업장 복
구에 따른 비용, 기타 사업 충당금 등 총 500억원 수준의 일회성 비용이 발생했
으며 영업외손익에서는 원화 강세에 따른 환손실이 주로 반영되었음

4Q26 및 27년 실적 추정치 상향 
1Q26에서 3Q26까지 이뤄지지 않았던 폴란드 EC2 K9 자주포 30문 인도는 
4Q26에 집중될 것으로 보이며 폴란드 EC1 천무 또한 기존 가이던스였던 26년 
천무 40대에서 60대로 인도 대수 상향이 이뤄짐. 이에 따라 4Q26 지상방산 영
업이익률은 22.8%로 회복을 전망. 27년에는 폴란드 K9 자주포 및 천무 인도 대
수 증가로 26년 수출 비중 58.0%에서 77.2%로 증가, 이에 따라 영업이익률 또
한 매출 믹스 개선에 따라 19.8%에서 28.0%로 개선을 전망

price pressures remain ﬁrm and reinforce our expectations for a

## Document 303: Chipping in_ how chips have become a huge driver of the dollar (2026-11-16)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Chipping in_ how chips have become a huge driver of the dollar (2026-11-16).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-11-16
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Chipping in: how chips have  
become a huge driver of the dollar

1 
Authors 
Mallika Sachdeva 
Strategist

November 16, 2026 
What do chips have to do with a new era of dollar dominance? As we discuss in 
this paper – a great deal. Chips are not only critical to the future of AI but have 
recently emerged as a huge driver of global trade and capital flows. 
It is well known that the US is dominant in upstream chip technology and 
downstream compute. This note delves deeper into the workings of the middle of 
the chain where flows have been reinforcing the dollar’s global role. The 
manufacturing of chips largely takes place in Asia where chip-related trade 
surpluses have ballooned. As we show, these surpluses are finding their way 
back to the US via investments in US capital markets. 
Asian chip surpluses are not being recycled into US Treasuries like petrodollar 
surpluses were, but into US equities. Foreign inflows into US equities have 
crossed USD1tn a year and become the mainstay of US deficit funding. The 
biggest source of increase over the past year has come from Asia. 
Equity flows have not just been supporting the US dollar but have provided 
financing to the companies driving the AI buildout and chips boom itself. This 
introduces forms of circularity and potential instability to global flows. Moreover, 
equities are a much riskier asset class than bonds – US stocks are three times as 
volatile as Treasuries, with tech stocks up to ten times as much. Growing dollar 
dependence on inherently riskier capital should spill into higher US dollar 
volatility over time. 
The rise of chips as a driver of dollar dominance can be seen as a symptom of 
change in the ultimate source of US power - from geopolitical to technological 
leadership. This power is increasingly concentrated in the private sector with 
dollar supportive flows intermediated via private balance sheets. 
Understanding this new framework and its risks is even more important given 
rapid shifts in news flow around AI, from the US political climate, high-stakes 
capital market raisings, potential tightening in export controls, and the highly 
consequential technology race with China.

### Page 2

Deutsche Bank AG Page 2

The rise of the chip dollar 
For the first time in modern history, global trade in semiconductors now 
exceeds trade in oil and gas (Figure 1) based on the four quarters of global 
exports till Q2-2026. More relevant from a currency perspective, is that chip 
surpluses in Asia ex-China have caught up to petroleum-related surpluses in the 
Gulf (Figure 2).  Why does this matter and what does this reflect?

Figure 1:  Global trade in chips has overtaken trade in 
oil & gas for the first time in history

Figure 2:  Asia (ex-China)’s chip surpluses are now as 
large as the Gulf’s petroleum-related surpluses

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, ITC Trade Map; Note: Oil & Gas are HS Codes 2709, 
2710, 2711.; Semiconductors is HS 8542

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, ITC Trade Map; Note: Oil & Gas are HS 2709, 2710, 
2711.; Semiconductors is HS 8542; Asia ex-China is Japan, Korea, Taiwan & ASEAN

We argue that the petrodollar is – rather quickly - being superseded by the chip-
dollar. This is both literal in terms of global surplus accumulation, but also 
archetypal. In other words, the petrodollar and chip-dollar are archetypes for two 
different states of the world, and for alternative foundations of dollar dominance.  
The petrodollar regime can be understood as one where US geopolitical 
leadership – over global security, international rules, and free trade – motivated 
global savings to be reinvested in the US sovereign via Treasury holdings. This 
cheapened funding for the US government which in turn supported the financing 
of US public goods provision.  
The emerging chip-dollar regime is one where US technology leadership is 
motivating global savings to be reinvested in US companies – providing funding 
for the US corporate sector to make enormous investments in new technology 
such as AI.  
The transition from the petrodollar to the chip-dollar is therefore a 
manifestation of change in the US’ leadership style - from geopolitical to 
technology leadership. This is not only changing the nature of global imbalances 
and capital flow recycling but is reshaping the foundations of dollar dominance.  
Revisiting the petrodollar regime  
After the 1971 Nixon shock, when the dollar’s convertibility to gold was 
terminated, we moved into a fully fiat currency world. The US dollar since then 
has been backed only by the fiscal credibility of the US government and the 
willingness of the world to finance US deficits by buying US assets.  
16 November 2026 
FX Special Report

### Page 3

Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

The 1974 petrodollar deal provided a crucial pillar of support for the dollar in its 
fiat infancy and sowed the seeds for a post-Bretton Woods regime in which US 
geopolitical leadership supported dollar dominance.  In 1974, in exchange for US 
security and military guarantees, Saudi Arabia agreed to price and invoice oil sales 
in USD. These USD denominated oil surpluses were then reinvested in US 
Treasuries. This was soon replicated across the Gulf. The 1990s and 2000s saw 
huge growth in commodity and manufacturing surpluses around the world. These 
were arguably supported by the US’s global backstop for trade and security – with 
surpluses saved in USD and recycled into US Treasuries via public balance sheets, 
in what can be considered a petrodollar-plus regime.  
We wrote earlier in the year how the Iran war could undermine the critical 
foundations of the petrodollar system by challenging the US security umbrella 
and shifting perceptions around US geopolitical leadership. We maintain that 
these changes are underway. But what has become apparent since then is that 
something else has been rising to take the petrodollar’s place in support of the 
dollar.  
The chip dollar is working differently  
While petrodollar surpluses were recycled into US Treasuries, chips surpluses 
are being recycled very differently.  
We focus on the chip surpluses being built in Asia ex-China. We note that China 
runs a very large semiconductor deficit, despite having the world’s largest overall 
trade surplus - something we will return to later. Asia’s chip surpluses are thus 
primarily hosted in Korea and Taiwan which are large semiconductor 
manufacturers, to a lesser extent in Japan which produces key materials, and 
ASEAN-10 which plays a role in assembly, packaging and testing.  
To explore how chip dollar surpluses are being recycled, we zoom in on South 
Korea – the epitome of the boom in chips trade.  
Korea’s semiconductor exports have grown over 200% YoY this year, with Korean 
chipmakers dominating in the manufacture of memory chips critical to AI 
acceleration. As Figure 3 shows, Korea’s semiconductor trade surplus has 
ballooned to close to USD250bn with 100% of Korea’s overall trade surplus now 
on account of chips alone.  
What is striking is that Korea’s chip surplus is being recycled abroad entirely via 
equities. Foreign sales of Korean equities have accounted for half the capital 
outflows in the past year, while Korean purchases of overseas equities account 
for the other half (Figure 3). Korean investments in overseas equities have been 
five times more than Korean purchases of overseas bonds in the last two years. In 
short, chip surpluses are being saved in global equities, not debt. 
The primary destination of Korea’s overseas equity purchases has been the US. 
While the US accounts for around 50% of global equity market capitalization, we 
note it is 70% of Korea’s overseas equity holdings (IMF CPIS). The US bias is even 
more exaggerated for Korean retail investors – a group empowered by access to 
financial technology. Korean households have 90% of their equity exposure in the 
US (Figure 4). It appears that North Asian capital is flowing back to the very US 
technology companies that are driving the chip surpluses themselves.

16 November 2026 
FX Special Report

### Page 4

Deutsche Bank AG Page 4

Figure 3:  Asian chip surpluses are being recycled into 
US equities – not US Treasuries like petrodollars were

Figure 4: 70% of Korea’s equity exposure abroad is to 
the US, and that figure is more than 90% for households

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, CEIC; Note: The semiconductor trade balance here is 
measured using Korea’s Customs Service classification for Semiconductors and 
equipment for semiconductor manufacturing

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Haver Analytics; Note: Data based on IMF’s 
Coordinated Portfolio Investment Survey as of end-2025

The reach for US equities by Asian retail is not just happening in Korea. In Japan, 
there have been significant allocations to US focused ETFs, encouraged by the 
NISA scheme. And as we have argued in our earlier paper, the US’s embrace of 
asset tokenization and the upcoming shift by US exchanges to 23/5 trading could 
further embolden and enable retail investors to access US equities. We could thus 
just be at the beginning of this new trend. Countries in North Asia like Korea and 
Japan not only have large savings pools, but also open capital accounts.  
This shift has shown up dramatically on the US capital account. Foreign 
purchases of US equities have exceeded USD1tn over the past year (Figure 5). 
Interestingly, most of the increase in purchases has come from Asia (Figure 6). 
Notably, foreign purchases of US equities are now more than double US Treasury 
allocations, which appear in relative decline. Even foreign purchases of US 
corporate bonds now exceed US Treasuries. This is notable considering the ramp 
up in hyper scaler issuance.

Figure 5: Foreign flows into US stocks now exceed 
flows into US Treasuries by a significant margin

Figure 6: Most of the increase in US equity purchases 
over the past year has come from Asia

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Haver Analytics (TICS data)

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Haver Analytics (TICS data)

16 November 2026 
FX Special Report

### Page 5

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5

Chips are the oil of the new economy  
The power of having oil priced and paid for in dollars was not just the recycling 
of petrodollar surpluses, but the value chain impact of having a key upstream 
resource dollarized. Oil is a critical input to almost every industrial process via 
petrochemicals – it is found in everything from plastic to paints, tires to 
cosmetics. It also plays a role in moving traded goods around the world as the 
transport fuel for ships. We would argue that having oil priced in dollars played a 
role in dollarizing global trade as companies looked to match the currency of 
downstream receivables to the upstream costs of oil. This is consistent with the 
Dominant Currency Paradigm (Gopinath et al, 2020).  
Chips arguably play a similar role in the digital and consumer economy as oil has 
played in the industrial economy. Chips are inputs to everything from consumer 
electronics, to automobiles, computers, communication devices, and most 
recently the bedrock of modern AI systems.  
Indeed, chips make up the lion’s share of spending in the AI buildout. Analysis by 
Epoch AI found that servers represented about 60% of the annualized cost of an 
indicative 1GW data centre in the US (Figure 7). According to the Semiconductor 
Industry Association (SIA), chips represent 95% of an AI server rack's value. In 
simple terms therefore, we can say chips make up over 50% of total capital 
expenditures for an AI data centre. Bloomberg Intelligence estimates hyper-
scaler capital expenditure commitments of close to USD8tn out to 2031 (Figure 
8), which would suggest a further USD4tn of chips demand from AI alone.

Figure 7: More than half the cost of the AI data centre 
build out is going towards chips

Figure 8:  Capital expenditure in the AI buildout may 
still have a long way to go

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Epoch AI; * Note: Based on a stylized model of a US 
hyperscaler AI data center that has 1 GW capacity. CapEx is converted to annual costs 
over each asset’s lifespan, assuming 5 years for IT equipment like chips.

Source: Deutsche Bank Research

To be sure, the market will remain sensitive to shifts in news flow and risks that 
the AI data centre buildout might slow whether due to the changing political 
climate in the US, calls for growing public oversight and safety monitoring of the 
industry, or a renewed focus on export controls.  
There are also open questions as to whether the boom in chips demand will be 
“one-off” as capacity is built or will be ongoing like with oil. Oil is a huge source of 
ongoing dollar flows primarily because of how consistent and trade-intensive it 
16 November 2026
 FX Special Report

### Page 6

Deutsche Bank AG Page 6

is: 100 million barrels are consumed daily with 70% of them being traded. We note 
that the replacement rate of chips in an average data centre is estimated at 3-7 
years, which suggests there could be ongoing demand even once the projected 
capex buildout reaches its zenith. But it remains to be seen whether the chips 
dollar can generate the type of enduring and stable recycling that the petrodollar 
has generated. 
Staying with the comparison between the petrodollar and chip-dollar, arguably it 
was US military presence in the world’s largest oil producing region in the 1970s 
– the Gulf - that gave the US control over the pricing of oil. What is it that gives 
the US control over the pricing and trade in chips? 
US control over chips: technology, economic rents, and supply chains   
The integrated circuit was invented in the US in 1958 with the microchip industry 
flourishing in California to become the well-known Silicon Valley. It is hard to 
overstate the continued dominance of the US in chips since then. Work by Sean 
Kenji Starrs of Kings College London maps the present-day dominance in 
compelling terms1. 
While most chip manufacturing happens in Asia – which is where the trade 
surpluses discussed above are being accumulated – most of the economic rents 
to chip-making accrues to US companies. Around 50% of the value in the 
semiconductor supply chain is in the design process where US companies control 
87% of the revenue (Starrs). The US has a near monopoly on the electronic design 
automation (EDA) software necessary for design, and controls around 60% of the 
revenue in semiconductor manufacturing equipment without which chips cannot 
be made. Materials, manufacturing, packaging and testing of chips happen 
outside the US, but these are worth only around 30% of the estimated value of 
the industry. Indeed, Starrs finds that 70% of the profits from the semiconductor 
industry flow to US firms (Figure 9).  
Furthermore, we note that all key countries involved in the supply chain of 
advanced chips remain US aligned. Japan plays an important role in materials, 
while Taiwan and Korea dominate in production (foundries). Importantly each of 
these North Asian players leans geopolitically towards the US over China. The US 
has arguably been less vocal about withdrawing economic support for security in 
North Asia than with NATO. One can thus posit the existence of a US-Asia chip-
dollar bloc. We note that the Netherlands has a monopoly on EUV lithography 
machines required to make the most advanced chips, but production of these 
machines in turn have a dependency on a US optical equipment firm helping 
grant the US crucial leverage even here.  
The potent combination of US control over key upstream technologies and 
intellectual property, alongside a supply chain located in US allies has given the 
US enormous control over chips. In recent years, the US has actively exercised 
this power via export controls to limit China’s access to US advanced chips and 
technologies. This has been the subject of books from Chip War by Chris Miller to 
Chokepoints by Edward Fishman. The importance of ensuring the integrity of US 
export controls was made apparent in recent comments by US Trade 
Representative Greer who noted that the US wants the “crown jewels” of the 
semiconductor industry to remain in the hands of the US or its allies. The US

1 Starrs, Sean Kenji. 2025. “Ringfencing Globalization: US Structural Power, Techno-Nationalism & Chinese Dependent 
Integration.” In Chapter 8 in Globalization in a Turbulent Era 
16 November 2026 
FX Special Report

### Page 7

Deutsche Bank AG Page 7

government has arguably had significant influence over the private sector sale of 
US chips technology and may double down on this after recent events at Nvidia 
to limit both direct access to US chips and the leasing of global data centers built 
with US chips to non-US allies (CNBC).

Figure 9: The US is very dominant in chips – controlling 
key upstream technology and an enormous part of the 
market value of the industry

Figure 10: China continues to run a large trade deficit in 
semiconductors – notable given China’s over USD2tn 
manufacturing goods surplus

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Starrs, Sean Kenji. 2025. “Ringfencing Globalization: 
US Structural Power, Techno-Nationalism & Chinese Dependent Integration.” In 
Chapter 8 in Globalization in a Turbulent Er; * Data for 2024 based on 40 chip 
companies in the Forbes 2000 ^ Data for 2000 based on 29 companies

Source: Deutsche Bank Research, ITC Trade Map (HS 8542)

Where does China fit in with all this?  
This note has looked at key changes in the industrial drivers of global imbalances 
(from fossil fuels to chips) and capital flows to the US (from debt to equities). 
Where does China – with the world’s biggest trade surplus – fit in?  
China now runs an over USD2 trillion manufactured goods surplus per year but 
continues to run a persistent semiconductor deficit (Figure 10). While US export 
controls limit access to the most advanced computing chips, China continues to 
import large volumes of memory and logic chips. Restrictions on access to 
advanced manufacturing equipment, particularly EUV lithography, have 
constrained China’s advances in domestic chip-making at the technological 
frontier, although greater self-sufficiency remains a major strategic priority. We 
note that the other drag on China’s trade position is commodities, mostly fossil 
fuels. Notably therefore, the remaining holes in China’s trade surplus are 
dominated by chip-dollar and petrodollar imports. In a forthcoming paper, we will 
look at China’s gains in renewable energy, new energy exports, and electrification, 
examining how these could emerge as long-term sources of power in both the AI 
race, and in increasing China’s independence from the dollar.  
The recycling of China’s manufactured goods surplus remains the topic of 
considerable debate. China’s surplus is in many ways the mirror of the US deficit, 
albeit with growing contribution from Europe, and increasingly intermediated 
through the Global South. There remains a school of thought that these Chinese 
savings find their way to the US through state banks and custodian balance 
sheets. But we note important points of difference with the chip dollar recycling 
we have discussed.  Nearly 50% of the net international assets held by Korea, 
16 November 2026 
FX Special Report

### Page 8

Deutsche Bank AG Page 8

Japan and Taiwan are in portfolio investments – namely equities and bonds. For 
China, this number is less than 20%. This is consistent with China’s capital account 
restrictions on private capital, as well as a growing geopolitical preference to limit 
exposure to US capital markets. Instead, China holds a much greater share of 
direct and other assets – reflecting growth in Chinese state-directed lending and 
acquisition of hard assets particularly in the Global South. On public reserve 
balance sheets, we note a growing preference for gold over dollar assets. Finally, 
we note a shift towards conversion of China’s surpluses, showing up in a stronger 
RMB and lower domestic rates.  
Bringing it together 
In the age of AI, it is well known that the US is dominant in both upstream chip 
technology as well as downstream computing. The US designs and controls 
supply chains for the world’s most advanced chips and is home to 10 times as 
many data centers as any other country (Stanford HAI). Both are sources of US 
power. This note has delved deeper into the workings of the middle of the chain 
where new flows are reinforcing the dollar’s global role in trade and capital flows. 
The manufacturing of high-end chips takes place in Asia ex-China and generates 
large trade surpluses there. This money is finding its way back to the US via 
Asian investments in US equities and corporate bonds. These capital flows are 
both funding the US trade deficit and providing financing to US companies that 
are driving the AI boom. Chips are thus ensuring the continued dominance of 
the dollar in global trade, payments, capital markets, and private sector savings, 
as well as supporting the US AI buildout seen as crucial to the future.  
There is an element of circularity to be conscious of: equity flows and financing 
for US AI related stocks are being funded out of Asian chip-related surpluses 
which are themselves being boosted by demand from US AI companies. This 
could introduce instability in global flows should demand be reassessed. For 
instance, if consumer demand for compute does not keep up with costly 
investments in supply, this would not just reduce earnings estimates for AI 
companies, but ultimately chip demand. One can see how this would challenge 
foreign equity financing for the US from both a push and pull perspective.  
Separately, as we noted in our last paper, proliferation of Chinese open-source 
models could also undermine the profitability of US companies and the 
attractiveness of US markets to global capital.  
The fact that the US dollar has become more dependent on foreign equity 
inflows than US Treasuries should make it inherently more volatile and exposed. 
Equities by nature are a riskier asset class than bonds. Recent realized volatility in 
S&P500 has been 3 times as much as in a US 10Y Treasuries. NASDAQ volatility is 
double that of the S&P and the Philly Semiconductor Index more than triple.  That 
US deficit financing is now dependent on capital flows into a more volatile asset 
class should spill over into currency volatility in time.  
In sum, US dollar dominance is now dependent on US technology leadership 
driven by private companies, and the flows underpinning the dollar are 
increasingly being intermediated on private sector balance sheets. A dollar driven 
by chips, AI and private capital connections should be inherently riskier.  
16 November 2026 
FX Special Report

### Page 9

Deutsche Bank AG Page 9

## Document 308: Cliff Notes_ the source and persistence of inflation (2026-11-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ the source and persistence of inflation (2026-11-20).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: the source and persistence of inflation
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
11:16 October 30 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia this week, all eyes were on the September CPI. Headline inflation rose 0.8% (4.3%yr) in
the month, largely owing to a 9.4% jump in fuel prices and a seasonal surge in domestic and
international holiday travel & accommodation. The underlying inflation pulse also proved hotter than

anticipated for Q3 overall, up 1.0% (3.7%yr).

### Page 2

While some parts of the basket are seeing disinflation – housing in particular looks to be cooling faster
than expected as builders lure sales with discounts – other components are still showing persistence.
Highlighting this is market services ex volatiles, which the RBA watches closely as an indicator of price
pressures related to domestic capacity. In Q3, this metric rose 1.2% (3.2%yr), and the monthly detail
points to momentum holding up – in line with our expectations for another chunky read in Q4.

With inflation running well above the RBA’s August forecasts, and some of the energy risks flagged
then materialising, we continue to expect the Monetary Policy Board will deliver a 25bp rate hike to
4.85% in November. Beyond November, another rate hike in February is a risk, but is not currently part
of our base case.
Across the Tasman, the RBNZ this week surprised market expectations for a 25bp rate hike, deciding
instead to wait for further information before taking the next step with policy. The decision was made

4:3, with the Governor in the ‘on hold’ camp. A 25bp hike in December to 3.00% and further tightening
in 2027 (Westpac NZ forecasts a peak of 4.00% in September) seems probable; however, each
decision will depend on fuel prices, inflation expectations and evidence of second-round pricing
pressures, along with the evolution of global economic risks.
In the US, the FOMC also held rates steady at its October meeting. Based on prior comments by key
members, the decision was not a surprise, and neither were Governor Cook and Cleveland Fed

President Hammack’s dissents in favour of a 25bp hike. The Committee remains positive on economic
activity, characterising it in the statement as “expanding at a strong pace” (an upgrade from “solid” in
September). Chair Warsh also subsequently emphasised the breadth of economic growth outside of
tech-related investment (evinced by Q3 GDP the day after; see below).
The Committee refrained from spotlighting key price risks in the statement. However, inflation “remains

elevated” and the FOMC are determined to achieve a “timely” and sustainable return of annual inflation
to 2.0%yr after 6 years above target. This guidance and the “accumulation of more information will help
the Committee determine the policy stance” suggests another 25bp hike is likely in December.
Thereafter, the FOMC will remain attentive to any broadening in inflation and related risks through
2027; although, in the absence of another shock, we expect them to remain on hold.

The Q3 GDP report certainly supported Chair Warsh’s view on the breadth of growth, the 3.4%
annualised gain for GDP benefitting from a 3.8% annualised increase in household consumption,
12.7% growth in non-residential investment as well as a robust increase in government spending.
Notably though, the contribution from business investment was effectively offset by net exports’ drag
given most of the componentry related to AI infrastructure investment is imported. This is not to say that

US firms won’t benefit from the investment once complete, but simply that much of the initial return
goes to the foreign businesses who provide the necessary capital equipment.
On consumption, the monthly PCE data implies downside risks remain for the consumer, the increase
in nominal spending seen in the month requiring dissaving and, on a real basis, consumption flat in the
month following a 0.5% gain in August. Cost-of-living pressures and uncertainty over the outlook is

### Page 3

Related articles
expected to see household consumption growth average around trend over the coming year, albeit with
considerable volatility quarter to quarter. This should see PCE inflation slow back towards its 2.0%yr
medium-term target as the FOMC desires, from 3.6%yr and 3.1%yr for the headline and core
measures respectively as of September.
Across the Atlantic, the ECB also left its key policy rates unchanged in October. Of note from the
meeting communications, energy prices continue to pose upside risks for inflation, but also downside

risks for growth. "While the full effects of the energy shock are still unfolding, underlying inflation has
remained broadly stable so far and wages do not show a material response". "Financing conditions
have [also] tightened", "which is likely to weigh on inflation and growth". In the press conference,
President Lagarde noted that, between the October and December meetings, a significant amount of
new economic information will be released, allowing forecasts and risks assessments to be tested.

Based on the October meeting communications, we continue to expect a rate hike at the December
meeting but believe this will be prove to be the last hike of the cycle. Note though, the spectrum of risks
Europe faces is broad and skewed towards inflation in the near term, hence the Council’s guidance will
remain hawkish. President Lagarde also announced today that she will step down as ECB President in
February ahead of the scheduled conclusion of her term next October.

## Document 359: Is the economy ‘plucked’_ (2026-11-21)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Is the economy ‘plucked’_ (2026-11-21).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-21
Pages: 31
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

A New Keynesian ‘plucking’ model of unemployment and inflation
PRELIMINARY AND INCOMPLETE
Anthony Brassil* and Callum Ryan*
Research Discussion Paper
20XX-YY
December 2025
*Economic Research Department
Reserve Bank of Australia
The views expressed in this paper are those of the authors and should not be attributed to the
Reserve Bank of Australia. Any errors are the sole responsibility of the authors.
Author: ryanca at domain rba.gov.au
External Communications: rbainfo@rba.gov.au

https://doi.org/10.47688/rdp20XX-YY

### Page 2

Abstract
We construct a New Keynesian model with search-and-matching frictions in the labour market that
can generate the nonlinear ‘plucking’ dynamics of unemployment in the data: unemployment tends to
spike upwards and then slowly decline. The model features endogenous layoffs, a ‘slippery’ job ladder,
downward nominal wage rigidity, and convex recruiting costs. It generates both the ‘deepness’ and
‘steepness’ asymmetries in unemployment dynamics, and broadly matches the response of labour-

market flows in a downturn. The model endogeneously generates a Phillips curve relationship that
is strongly nonlinear and state-dependent, and a short-run non-accelerating rate of unemployment
(NAIRU) that fluctuates asymmetrically over the business cycle. We also show that cyclical properties
(e.g. shock volatility, nominal rigidities, policy regime) can have a significant effect on the long-run
NAIRU via agents’ precautionary response to future uncertainty.

JEL Classification Numbers:
Keywords:

### Page 4

Figure 1: Unemployment rate in Australia
*Long-run NAIRU estimate from Ballantyne and Cusbert (2025).
1. Introduction
The unemployment rate in the data has strongly nonlinear dynamic (Figure 1). It spikes sharply in
occasional downturns, then declines slowly but persistently in recoveries.1 It is right-skewed in both
levels (i.e. asymmetric ‘deepness’) and in changes (i.e. asymmetric ‘steepness’) (Sichel 1993). Gross
flows between unemployment and employment display similar nonlinearities. Standard search-and-

matching models of the labour market struggle to generate these nonlinearities in unemployment
and labour-market flows (Ferraro 2023).
If this asymmetric pattern stems from frictions within the labour market, what implication do those
frictions have for the relationship between inflation and unemployment? Research using nonlinear
structural models to explore the asymmetric dynamics in the labour market tend to abstract from price
frictions and inflation dynamics (e.g. Dupraz, Nakamura and Steinsson 2025; Ferraro 2018; Petrosky-

Nadeau, Zhang and Kuehn 2018), while in the New Keynesian literature, the usual approach of
linearising equilibrium conditions excludes these nonlinear dynamics by assumption. The 2020s
inflation surge has reignited research into the nonlinearity of the Phillips curve, and the extent to which
this nonlinearity stems from frictions within the labour market (e.g. Benigno and Eggertsson 2023).
But this literature tends not to model the labour market in as much detail.

In this paper, we explore the extent to which a detailed search-and-matching model of the labour
market with pricing frictions can match the nonlinear dynamics in the labour market. We then use
the model to examine the implications of these nonlinearities for the Phillips curve relationship and
the non-accelerating rate of unemployment (NAIRU).
1 Friedman (1964) uses the ‘plucking’ description for the asymmetric way in which output seems to be occasionally ‘plucked’

below trend, before recovering back to trend.

### Page 5

2
We start with a general-equilibrium search-and-matching model similar to Bloesch, Lee and
Weber (2024), in which firms post wages and vacancies and set prices (subject to nominal rigidities
and recruiting costs), and workers search (while unemployed and on the job) and decide whether to
accept and quit jobs. Policy steers aggregate demand to balance inflation and unemployment targets.
We extend this model to include endogenous layoffs, downward nominal wage rigidity, and a ‘slippery’

job ladder (drawing on Dupraz et al (2025)).2 We find that these features - together with convex
recruiting costs - are collectively key to achieving both the asymmetric spikes in unemployment when
shocks occur and the slow speed in the subsequent recovery. The model is sufficiently detailed to
broadly match the dynamics of unemployment and gross flows, but tractable enough that we can
obtain the global nonlinear solution with rational expectations and aggregate uncertainty. This is

important because agents’ precautionary response to future uncertainty is a significant determinant
of outcomes, especially the long-run NAIRU.
We show that the model can generate substantial asymmetry in both ‘deepness’ and ‘steepness’
(comparable to or greater than in the data). The response of unemployment, job-finding, and
separations after a stylised negative demand shock broadly matches the response of these variables in
downturns over the past 50 years. Aggregate demand shocks have an especially asymmetric effect,

because the effect on inflation amplifies the effect of the downward nominal wage rigidity. When
supply shocks occur, the inflation response somewhat eases the downward nominal wage constraint.
The model endogenously generates a Phillips curve relationship that is strongly nonlinear and state-
dependent. Conditional on some initial state of the economy, the Phillips curve is much steeper for
low rates of unemployment. When the initial unemployment rate is higher, the Phillips curve shifts to

the right, and when the initial level of real wages is higher, it shifts upwards.
We can also use the model to simulate a ‘short-run NAIRU’, which is the unemployment rate consistent
with eliminating inflationary pressure from the labour market at some cyclical horizon (e.g. 1-2 years).
This short-run NAIRU varies asymmetrically with the state of the economy. It tends to rise when
unemployment is high, but also when unemployment has fallen rapidly (because real wages increase).

Finally, we can use the ‘stochastic steady state’ of the model to explore the determinants of the long-
run NAIRU, i.e. the unemployment rate consistent with stable inflation in the absence of shocks (but
where agents still expect that shocks may occur in the future). We find that when firms account for
the interaction between aggregate risk and asymmetric wage adjustment costs in their wage-setting
decisions, it generates a trade-off between unemployment variance and steady-state unemployment.

For example, if policy places greater weight on reducing the gap between unemployment and the
long-run NAIRU (instead of the gap between inflation and target), it tends to increase the long-run
NAIRU. This is because by mitigating fluctuations in labour demand, policy reduces the risk of hitting
downward nominal wage rigidities. Firms are then more willing to set higher real wages in steady
state, which reduces the optimal amount of employment in steady state.

The remainder of this introduction discusses the literature. Section 2 outlines the model. Section 3
evaluates the extent to which it can match the nonlinear dynamics in labour-market data. Section 4
considers the implications for the Phillips curve and NAIRU.
2 By ‘slippery’ job ladder, we mean that workers recently hired out of unemployed are much more likely to be laid off.

### Page 6

3
Literature review. The cyclical asymmetries in unemployment are established empirically by e.g.
Sichel (1993), McKay and Reis (2008), and Dupraz et al (2025). Ferraro (2023) analyses the ability of
the baseline Diamond-Mortensen-Pissarides (DMP) model to produce these asymmetries, and finds
that it cannot generate the ‘steepness’ asymmetry (i.e. right-skewness in unemployment changes).
Petrosky-Nadeau et al (2018) finds that the DMP model can generate the ‘deepness’ asymmetry given

large enough shocks.
Dupraz et al (2025) and Ferraro (2018) propose search-and-matching models that can generate
sharp spikes and slow declines in unemployment. We take several features from Dupraz et al (2025),
including downward nominal wage rigidity and a ‘slippery’ job ladder. Pizzinelli, Theodoridis and
Zanetti (2020) explore the state-dependence of unemployment fluctuations in a model with
endogenous layoffs and on-the-job search.3 Adjemian, Karamé and Langot (2021) estimate a

nonlinear model with endogenous separations, downward wage rigidity, worker heterogeneity and
segmented markets, and show that it can generate similar asymmetries in unemployment, hiring,
and separations to the data.
Our definition of a ‘short-run NAIRU’ is similar to the concept discussed by Hall and Kudlyak (n.d.), who
argue that the natural rate of unemployment may vary cyclically and track the actual unemployment
rate during recoveries. Hall and Kudlyak (2022) discusses the ability of DMP-style models to generate

the slow persistent recovery in unemployment that occurs after recessions. Fujita and Ramey (2007)
show that adding vacancy adjustment costs to a DMP model can produce a more sluggish response
for vacancies and hiring after a downturn, slowing down the recovery in unemployment. Mercan,
Schoefer and Sedláček (2024) suggest a congestion mechanism, whereby new hires and incumbent
workers are imperfect substitutes in production, which also propagates unemployment after a shock.

This literature above generally abstracts from price rigidities and inflation dynamics. There is a
substantial literature incorporating labour-market frictions into New Keynesian models (see e.g.
Blanchard and Galí 2010). Recent examples include Moscarini and Postel-Vinay (2023), Faccini and
Melosi (2025), and Bloesch et al (2024). These papers focus on the role of on-the-job search and wage
competition between firms in inflation dynamics. We adopt a similar model for wage setting, drawing

on the tractable formulation in Bloesch et al (2024). But these papers use linear approximations.
The literature on downward nominal wage rigidity explores the extent to which it generates a
nonlinear Phillips curve and asymmetric cyclical dynamics (see e.g. Kim and Ruge-Murcia 2009;
Abbritti and Fahr 2013; Daly and Hobijn 2014; Benigno and Antonio Ricci 2011; Schmitt-Grohé
and Uribe 2022; Benigno and Eggertsson 2023; Benigno and Eggertsson 2024). Lepetit (2020)

also constructs a New Keynesian model with asymmetric unemployment dynamics, although the
asymmetry stems from the convex relationship between the job-finding rate and unemployment.
This literature does not model the labour market in as much detail (e.g. endogenous layoffs, on-the-
job search, job ladder) and does not seek to match the ‘plucking’ property in unemployment to the
same extent.
3 Fujita and Ramey (2012) also analyse versions of the DMP model with endogenous separations and on-the-job search,

but their focus is not on asymmetries in unemployment rate dynamics

### Page 7

4
2. Model
The model consists of firms, workers, and a policy rule. We start by outlining the firms’ problem
and optimal choices. Firms’ optimal decision-making drives most of the model’s mechanics. We then
discuss workers and the policy regime, which are modelled more simply. 5 contains a full list of
equilibrium conditions, as well as details of the calibration and solution method.
2.1 Firms
Firms make decisions around hiring, separations, and price- and wage-setting.

Hiring: Firms choose how many vacancies to post, V i t . These vacancies generate Mi t = qtV i t matches,
where qt is determined by a matching function and depends on aggregate matching efficiency and
labour market tightness. A proportion 1 − ϕ E t of these matches are unemployed searchers (where ϕ E t
is the share of unemployed searchers in total searchers, weighted by search intensity).
Firms must screen matches. Then the individual productivities of employed matches are revealed.

Firms make offers to those with productivities above some threshold xi t , which is 1−F i t of them (where
F i t = F(xi t ) and F is the CDF of the individual productivity distribution). For unemployed matches,
firms have no information about their individual productivity and make offers to all of them. This
feature proxies for the idea that firms may be more uncertain about the capabilities of unemployed
applicants.
Matches with offers choose whether to accept them; Ace t (W i t ) and employed matches and Acu t (W i t )

of unemployed matches do so, where W i t is the real wage paid by the firm. These job-acceptance
probabilities are characterised in the description of workers below.
The number of new hires is given by
Hi t = ϕ E t Mi t (1 − F i t )Ace t (W i t ) + (1 − ϕ E t )Mi t Acu t (W i t ).
Define Hi,u t = (1 − ϕ E t )Mi t Acu t (W i t ) as the number of hires from unemployment.
Recruiting costs include a per-vacancy cost and a per-match ‘screening’ cost:

Separations: There are two types of separation: quits and layoffs. Each period, Qt (W i t ) of firms’
existing employees quit (either to unemployment or to another job). Just as firms’ hiring rate is

increasing in the real wage they offer (via the job-acceptance probabilities Ace t (W i t ) and Acu t (W i t )),
their quits rate is decreasing in the real wage by the firm. This function is characterised in the section
below on workers.
Workers’ probability of being laid off depends on whether they are insecurely or securely attached.
Insecurely attached employees are all those who have just been hired out of unemployed in
the previous period. Before aggregate shocks are revealed, some portion of new hires from

### Page 8

5
unemployment, δ u, (i.e. who were hired the previous period), draw a new productivity from
distribution G. Those whose productivities are less than some threshold xi,m t−1 are laid off, which
is Gi t−1 of them (where Gi t = G(xi,m t−1) and G is the CDF of the distribution).4 All insecurely attached
employees who are not laid off then because securely attached (together with all those who have
been employed for more than one period).
After aggregate shocks are revealed and quitting decisions made, δ of securely attached employees

draw a new individual productivity. Firms then decide to lay off those whose productivities are less
than some threshold xi t , which is F i t of them (where F i t = F(xi t ) and F is the CDF of the individual
productivity distribution). The layoff probability for securely attached employees will be lower than
for insecurely attached employees both because δ < δ u and the distribution F has less mass at low
values than G.

Production: Firms’ production function is Y i t = At Li t .
Price and wage-setting: Firms set a (real) price Pi t subject to the demand schedule Y i t = Pi t −θ Yt

4 Note on timing: These layoffs have to occur before the revelation of new shocks, otherwise the number of hires from

2.2 Workers
All workers who are unemployed when shocks are revealed search for jobs. A share λ EE of employed
workers also search. The total number of searchers is then λ EE ˜Nt−1 + 1 − ˜Nt−1, and the effective
share of employed searchers is

Those who enter unemployment after shocks are revealed, either because they are laid off or because

they quit to unemployment, do not search in that period.6
A share λ EU of employed workers consider quitting to unemployment.
6 This means that even if hiring costs went to zero, unemployment would remain above zero. If the newly unemployed
were allowed to search, this would raise the average number of searchers, especially in periods where layoffs are high.
Workers hired last period who are laid off before shocks are revealed can search. This is to avoid Hu t−1 or Nt−1 being a

state variable.

Monetary policy: Policy tries to implement a flexible inflation target, but is subject to various shocks
(e.g. it cannot fully offset all demand shocks immediately). Therefore:
ln Πt − ln Πtarget + γ p ( Nt − Ntarget ) = ln Mt

where Mt is an exogenous nominal demand shock.
2.4 Equilibrium, solution and calibration
We assume that firms have rational expectations, which implies that they understand the interaction
between nonlinearities in the economy and aggregate risk when forming their expectations. This
means, for example, that in the presence of downward nominal wage rigidity, firms will set lower
wages even in steady state, given the risk that it will be very costly for them to reduce wages if

negative shocks occur in the future.
We compute the global solution to the nonlinear model, so that we can explore how the interaction
between nonlinearities and aggregate risk affects expectations and dynamics. The state variables are
employment ˜Nt−1 (after insecurely attached employees are laid off), real wages Wt−1, the average
idiosyncratic productivity of employees ¯xt−1, and the aggregate productivity and demand shocks, At
and Mt .
The model is calibrated so that steady-state values for unemployment and key labour-market flows

are broadly similar to their values Australian data around 2025, as well as to match the standard
deviation of the unemployment rate over 1980 to 2019 and generate a similar positive skewness of
unemployment rate changes.
See Appendix 5 for more detail on the calibration and solution method.
3. How well can we match the dynamics of the labour market in the data?
3.1 Comparing the model to labour-market dynamics in the data
In this section, we compare the dynamics of unemployment and labour-market flows in the model

to Australian data. Figure 2 illustrates the path of transition rates and flows between labour-market
states in Australia since 1980. It shows that the UE rate is negative correlated with unemployment,
even though UE flows (as a proportion of the labour force) are positively correlated. UE flows increase
when the pool of unemployed is larger, but this increase is not enough for the UE rate to increase.

### Page 14

11
Figure 2: Labour-market transition rates and flows in Australia
Note: The UE and EU flows in the data are divided by the labour force size to make them comparable to
the model flows. The shaded regions represent contractions, which are identified using the algorithm in
Dupraz et al (2025).
Figure 3: Comparing recessions in the model and the data
Note: The UE and EU flows in the data are divided by the labour force size to make them comparable to the model

flows.
The figure also shows that the EU rate and EU flows are postively correlated with unemployment,
and the EE rate (i.e. job-to-job transition rate) is negatively correlated (at a cyclical frequency).
Figure 3 compares a stylised recession in our model to recessions in the data since 1980.9 The model
downturn is generated with a sequence of six demand shocks (over six months) calibrated to produce
a similar path for unemployment to the downturns in the data. The paths for transition rates and

flows from the model are broadly similar to the data. In particular, the UE rate falls while UE flows
increase, and the magnitude of all responses is comparable to the data.
9 We exclude the 2010-14 and 2022-25 upswings because they involved a gradual increase in unemployment rather than
a spike.

### Page 15

12
Next, we consider whether our model can reproduce the ‘plucking’ or asymmetric ‘sharp spike’ and
‘slow decline’ patterns we see in the unemployment rate data. One measure of this pattern is the
skewness of unemployment rate changes. In the data, this moment is quite positive, reflecting
frequent small decreases and occasional large increases (Table 1) (Sichel (1993) called this a
‘steepness’ asymmetry). Standard search-and-matching models tend to generate negative skewness

in unemployment changes (Ferraro 2023). In contrast, under the current calibration, simulations of
our model generate a substantially positive skewness of unemployment rate changes, although not
quite as large as in the data.
We can also compare other moments of the unemployment rate and moments of the unemployment-
to-employment (UE) and employment-to-unemployment (EU) transition rates in our model to the
data. The model generates somewhat higher variance and negative skewness in the UE rate, whereas

the UE rate is positively skewed in Australian data over this sample period. In US data, the UE rate
has higher variance and is negatively skewed (see Appendix A.3). The model matches the variance
and skewness of the EU rate well, but generates more skewness in changes in the EU rate than in
the data.
Table 1: Comparing moments of quarterly unemployment and transition rates
Unemployment rate UE rate EU rate

Skewness 1.12 0.59 -0.38 0.40 0.52 0.57
Skewness of changes 1.37 1.71 -0.74 -0.62 2.02 0.69
Notes: These moments use Australian data from 1980 to 2019, detrended using estimates from Ballantyne and Cusbert (2025). See
Appendix A.3 for moments using undetrended data and using US data.
3.2 Explaining the model’s dynamics
The IRFs in Figure 4 demonstrate why we can match these moments. The responses are
strongly nonlinear. Large negative shocks generate a large increase in the unemployment rate

(disproportionately more than small negative shocks), whereas positive shocks do not reduce it very
much. Unemployment recovers very slowly after an initial spike.
To explore why the model is able to generate these asymmetric dynamics, Table 2 compares the
model’s moments when three key frictions are removed, either one at a time or all together. First, the
downward nominal wage rigidity is removed by making wage adjustment costs symmetric. The next

column removes insecurely attached workers, so that all workers have the same layoff rate. The next
column removes convexity in recruiting costs, making them (almost) linear. The final column removes
all three frictions. In each case, we recalibrate the model so that it generates the same steady-state
unemployment rate and flows, and the same variance in unemployment. Figure 5 compares the
response of each version of the model to positive and negative demand shocks.

When all three frictions are removed (the last column in Table 2), the degree of asymmetry
declines and the skewness of unemployment rate changes is much lower. Unlike the standard DMP
model (Ferraro 2023), the skewness of unemployment changes remains positive, likely because
layoffs are still endogenous, which generates some asymmetry. Figure 5 shows that unemployment
recovers more rapidly, especially the UE rate. The value of a match is a forward-looking object,

determined largely by expected future surplus, and therefore rises quickly after the shock peaks.

### Page 16

13
Figure 4: Response to aggregate demand shocks
Note: The economy is initially at steady state.
When unemployment is high, the vacancy-filling rate is also high, so marginal recruiting costs fall.
Unemployed searchers make up a much larger proportion of total searchers, so for a given amount
of hiring, hires out of unemployment are higher.
